n
, proving the upper bound
(ii)
.
∎

Proof of (
S.93
).

Note immediately that

H_{\mathrm{Vor}}(x,y)\leq\int_{L\cap\Omega}\exp(-np_{x}(z))\,dz\leq\int_{L}\exp(-n\mu_{d}p_{\min}\|x-z\|^{d}/2d)\,dz.

We have already analyzed this integral in the proof
of (
S.92
), with the analysis implying that

\int_{L}\exp(-n\mu_{d}p_{\min}\|x-z\|^{d}/2d)\,dz=\frac{\eta_{d-2}(2d)^{\frac{d-1}{d}}}{(np_{\min})^{(d-1)/d}}K_{\mathrm{Vor}}\biggl(\frac{\|y-x\|}{(2dn/p_{\min})^{1/d}}\biggr).

This is exactly (
S.93
) with
C_{3}=\eta_{d-2}(2d/p_{\min})^{(d-1)/d}
and
C_{4}=(2d/p_{\min})^{1/d}
.
∎

G.2.4
Compact kernel approximation

The kernel function
H_{\mathrm{Vor}}(x,y)
is not compactly supported, and in
our analysis it will frequently be convenient to approximate it by a compactly
supported kernel. The following lemma does the trick. Let
\varepsilon_{0}:=(\log n/n)^{1/d}
.

Lemma S.18
.

Let
x,y\in\Omega
, and
L=\{z:\|x-z\|=\|y-x\|\}
. For any
a,c>0
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
a,c
and
d
such
that

\int_{L\cap\Omega}\exp(-cn\|x-z\|^{d})\,dz\leq C\biggl(\frac{1\{\|x-y\|\leq C\varepsilon_{0}\}}{n^{(d-1)/d}}+\frac{1}{n^{a}}\biggr)

(S.96)

where
\varepsilon_{0}:=(\log n/n)^{1/d}
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}=C_{1}\varepsilon_{0}
for
C_{1}=(a/c)^{1/d}
. The
key is that if
\|x-z\|\geq\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}
, then

\exp(-cn\|x-z\|^{d})\leq\frac{1}{n^{a}}.

Now suppose
\|y-x\|>2\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}
. Then
\|x-z\|\geq\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}
for all
z\in L
, and

\int_{L\cap\Omega}\exp(-cn\|x-z\|^{d})\,dz\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(L\cap\Omega)}{n^{a}}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{L\cap\Omega}\exp(-cn\|x-z\|^{d})\,dz

\displaystyle\leq 1\{\|y-x\|\leq 2\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}\}\int_{L\cap\Omega}\exp(-cn\|x-z\|^{d})\,dz+\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(L\cap\Omega)}{n^{a}}

\displaystyle\leq 1\{\|y-x\|\leq 2\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}\}\int_{B_{d-1}((x+y)/2,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0})}\exp(-cn\|x-z\|^{d})\,dz+2\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(L\cap\Omega)}{n^{a}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1\{\|y-x\|\leq 2\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}\}}{n^{(d-1)/d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\exp(-\|z\|^{d})\,dz+2\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(L\cap\Omega)}{n^{a}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\biggl(\frac{1\{\|y-x\|\leq 2\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}\}}{n^{(d-1)/d}}+\frac{1}{n^{a}}\biggr).

for
C_{2}=\max\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\exp(-\|z\|^{d})\,dz,2\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(L\cap\Omega)\}
.
Equation (
S.96
) follows upon taking

C=\max\{2C_{1},C_{2}\}
.
∎

G.3
Proofs of technical lemmas for Theorem
5

G.3.1
Proofs of Lemmas
S.7
and
S.8

Proof of Lemma
S.7
.

For each
k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell)
and
\bf{i}\in\mathcal{I}
, it follows from
Hölder’s inequality that

|\theta_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(f)|\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\|\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}=\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}2^{-\ell d/2},

and taking supremum over
k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell)
and
\bf{i}\in\mathcal{I}
gives
the result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
S.8
.

The proof hinges on an application of an integration by parts
identity (
S.97
), valid for all
f\in C^{1}(\Omega)
. We thus
first derive (
S.57
) for all
f\in C^{1}(\Omega)
, before
returning to complete the full proof.

Now, taking
f\in C^{1}(\Omega)
, a simple calculation verifies that for each

i=1,\ldots,d
, and all
\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}
such that
{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}
, we have

\int_{\Omega}f(x)\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x)\,dx=-\int_{\Omega}D_{i}fI_{i}\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x)\,dx.

(S.97)

Here
I_{1}f(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x_{1}}f((t,x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}))\,dt
is the
partial integral operator in the
1
st coordinate, and
I_{i}
are defined
likewise. Now, we introduce some notation: for all
x,y\in\Omega
, and for
each
i=1,\ldots,d
, take

K_{\ell}^{i}(x,y)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell),{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}\Psi_{\ell,k}^{{\bf i}}(x)\Psi_{\ell,k}^{{\bf i}}(y),\quad\Lambda_{\ell}^{i}(x,y)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell),{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}\Psi_{\ell,k}^{{\bf i}}(x)I_{i}\Psi_{\ell,k}^{{\bf i}}(y).

By definition,
K_{\ell}^{i}
is the integral operator such that

P_{\ell,i}f(x):=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell),{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}\theta_{k\ell}^{{\bf i}}(f)\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x)=\int f(y)K_{\ell}^{i}(x,y)\,dy,

and we may use the integration by parts identity (
S.97
) to
obtain

P_{\ell,i}f=-\int D_{i}f(y)\Lambda_{\ell}^{i}(\cdot,y)\,dy.

(S.98)

Taking absolute value, integrating over
\Omega
and applying Fubini’s
theorem, we determine that

\displaystyle\|P_{\ell,i}f\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}|D_{i}f(y)||\Lambda_{\ell}^{i}(x,y)|\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\|D_{i}f\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\cdot\sup_{y\in\Omega}\|\Lambda_{\ell}^{i}(\cdot,y)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|D_{i}f\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\cdot\sup_{y\in\Omega}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell),{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}\|\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\cdot|I_{i}\Psi_{\ell,k}^{{\bf i}}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d}2^{-\ell}\|D_{i}f\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}.

Now, for each
i=1,\ldots,d
, take

\theta_{\ell\cdot}^{i}(f)=\bigl(\theta_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(f):k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell),{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}\bigr),

where
\mathcal{I}_{i}\subset\mathcal{I}
contains all indices
{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}
for
which
{\bf i}_{j}=0
for all
j<i
, and
{\bf i}_{i}=1
.

Using the
L^{2}(\Omega)
orthogonality property of the Haar basis and applying
Hölder’s inequality gives

\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}^{i}(f)\|_{1}=\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}^{i}(P_{\ell,i}f)\|_{1}\leq\|P_{\ell,i}f\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\cdot\|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K},{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 2^{{\ell d/2}}\|P_{\ell,i}f\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}.

and summing up over
i=1,\ldots,d
gives the desired upper bound on

\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}(f)\|_{1}
.

Finally, a density argument will imply the same result holds for any
f\in\mathrm{BV}(\Omega)
. In particular, there exists a sequence
\{f_{n}\}\subset C^{1}(\Omega)
for which

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|f_{n}-f\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\to 0,\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{TV}(f_{n};\Omega)=\mathrm{TV}(f;\Omega),

(S.99)

see, e.g.,
Evans and Gariepy 2015
; consequently

\|\theta_{k\cdot}(f)\|_{1}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\theta_{k\cdot}(f_{n})\|_{1}\leq d2^{d}2^{\ell(1-d/2)}\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{TV}(f_{n};\Omega)=d2^{d}2^{\ell(1-d/2)}\mathrm{TV}(f;\Omega),

and the proof is complete upon taking
C_{1}=d2^{d}
.
∎

G.3.2
Proof of Proposition
S.2

The proof of Proposition
S.2
follows in
spirit the analysis of
(
Donoho et al. 1995
)
. First we upper bound the

\ell^{2}
-loss by the sum of two terms, a modulus of continuity and tail
width, then we proceed to separately upper bound each term. The primary
difference between our situation and that of
(
Donoho et al. 1995
)
is
that we are working with respect to intersections of Besov bodies rather than
Besov bodies themselves.

For the rest of this proof, take
\widehat{\theta}=\widehat{\theta}^{(\lambda,\ell^{\ast})}
and
\theta_{0}=\theta(f_{0})
.

Step 1: Upper bound involving modulus of continuity and
tail width.
We are going to establish that

\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\|_{2}\leq\Omega\Bigl(\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(2M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(2L),\epsilon_{n}+\lambda\Bigr)+\Delta\Bigl(\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(L),\ell^{\ast}\Bigr),

(S.100)

where
\Omega(\Theta,\epsilon)
is the modulus of continuity

\Omega(\Theta,\epsilon):=\sup_{\theta,\theta\in\Theta}\bigl\{\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}:\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon\bigr\},

(S.101)

and
\Delta(\Theta,\ell)
is the tail width

\Delta(\Theta,\ell):=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\theta-\theta_{\leq\ell}\|_{2}.

(S.102)

Observe that by the triangle inequality,

\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\|_{2}\leq\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta_{0,\leq\ell^{\ast}}\|_{2}+\|\theta_{0}-\theta_{0,\leq\ell^{\ast}}\|_{2}.

The second term on the right hand side of the previous expression is upper
bounded by
\Delta\bigl(\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(L),\ell^{\ast}\bigr)
. We turn now to upper bounding
the first term by the modulus of continuity. Observe that for each index, we
are in one of two situations: either

|\widetilde{\theta}_{\ell,k}^{{\bf i}}|<\lambda\Longrightarrow|\widehat{\theta}_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}|=0~~\quad\textrm{and}~~|\widehat{\theta}_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}-\theta_{0,\ell k}^{{\bf i}}|=|{\theta}_{0,\ell k}^{{\bf i}}|\leq\lambda+\epsilon_{n},

or

|\widetilde{\theta}_{\ell,k}^{{\bf i}}|\geq\lambda\Longrightarrow|\theta_{0,\ell k}^{{\bf i}}|\geq|\widetilde{\theta}_{\ell,k}^{{\bf i}}|-\epsilon_{n}\geq\frac{1}{2}|\widetilde{\theta}_{\ell k}|=\frac{1}{2}|\widehat{\theta}_{\ell k}|~~\textrm{and}~~|\widehat{\theta}_{\ell k}-\theta_{0,\ell k}^{{\bf i}}|\leq\epsilon_{n}.

It follows that for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\},k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell)
, we have
|\widehat{\theta}_{\ell k}|\leq 2|\theta_{0,\ell k}^{{\bf i}}|
, and so
\widehat{\theta}\in\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(2M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(2L)
. Moreover, the above calculations also confirm
that
\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta_{0,\leq\ell^{\ast}}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda+\epsilon_{n}
. Thus

\|\widehat{\theta}-\theta_{0,\leq\ell^{\ast}}\|_{2}\leq\Omega\bigl(\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(2M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(2L),\epsilon_{n}+\lambda),

establishing (
S.100
).

Step 2: Tail width.

Fix
\theta\in\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(L)
.
For each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
we have

\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}\|_{1}\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{1}2^{-\ell}LM,

with the first inequality following from Hölder, and the second
inequality from Lemmas
S.7
and
S.8
.
Summing over
\ell=\ell^{\ast}+1,\ell^{\ast}+2,\ldots
gives

\|\theta-\theta_{\leq\ell^{\ast}}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{\ell=\ell^{\ast}+1}^{\infty}\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2C_{1}LM2^{-\ell^{\ast}},

and it follows that

\Delta(\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(L),\ell^{\ast})\leq\sqrt{2C_{1}LM}2^{-\ell^{\ast}/2}.

(S.103)

Step 3: Modulus of continuity.

Fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(2M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(2L)
such that
\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
. For each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
we have

\displaystyle\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}-\theta_{\ell\cdot}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\min\{2^{\ell d}\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}-\theta_{\ell\cdot}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{2},\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}-\theta_{\ell\cdot}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}-\theta_{\ell\cdot}^{\prime}\|_{1}\}

(S.104)

\displaystyle\leq\min\{2^{\ell d}\epsilon^{2},2C_{1}\epsilon L2^{\ell(d/2-1)},4C_{1}ML2^{-\ell}\},

with the final inequality following from the triangle inequality, i.e.

\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|\leq\|\theta\|+\|\theta^{\prime}\|
, and
Lemmas
S.7
and
S.8
.

The three upper bounds in (
S.104
) divide
\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
into three zones, based on the indices
\ell
for which each
upper bound is tightest.

•

The first
dense
zone contains
\ell=0,\ldots,\lceil\log_{2}(2C_{1}L/\epsilon)\cdot\frac{2}{2+d}\rceil=:N_{1}
. In the dense
zone, the extremal vectors
\theta
are dense, i.e. everywhere non-zero.

•

The second
intermediate
zone contains
\ell=N_{1}+1,\ldots,\lceil\log_{2}(2M/\epsilon)\cdot\frac{2}{d}\rceil=:N_{2}
. In the
intermediate zone, the extremal vectors
\theta
are neither dense nor
sparse.

•

The third
sparse
zone contains
\ell=N_{2}+1,\ldots
. In
the sparse zone, the extremal vectors are sparse, i.e. they have exactly
one non-zero entry.

Summing over
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
gives

\displaystyle\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\|\theta_{\ell\cdot}-\theta_{\ell\cdot}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_{1}}2^{\ell d}+2C_{1}L\epsilon\sum_{\ell=N_{1}+1}^{N_{2}}2^{-\ell(1-d/2)}+4MC_{1}L\sum_{\ell=N_{2}+1}^{\infty}2^{-\ell},

(S.105)

where we use the convention that
\sum_{\ell=N_{1}+1}^{N_{2}}\cdot=0
if

N_{1}\geq N_{2}
. There are three terms on the right hand side
of (
G.3.2
), and we derive upper bounds on each.

•

For the first term, recalling that
\sum_{\ell=0}^{N}a^{\ell}=\frac{a^{N+1}-1}{a-1}
for any
a>1
, and noting that
\lceil b\rceil\leq b+1
, we have

\epsilon^{2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_{1}}2^{\ell d}=\epsilon^{2}\Bigl(\frac{(2^{d})^{N_{1}+1}-1}{2^{d}-1}\Bigr)\leq 4^{d}\epsilon^{2}\Bigl(\frac{2C_{1}L}{\epsilon}\Bigr)^{2/d}=4^{d}(2C_{1})^{2/d}L^{2/d}\epsilon^{4/(2+d)}.

•

For the second term, we obtain separate upper bounds depending on
whether
d=2
or
d\geq 3
: when
d=2
,

2C_{1}L\epsilon\sum_{\ell=N_{1}+1}^{N_{2}}2^{-\ell(1-d/2)}=2C_{1}L\epsilon(N_{2}-(N_{1}+1))_{+}\leq 2C_{1}L\epsilon N_{2}\leq 2C_{1}L\epsilon\bigl(\log_{2}(2M/\epsilon)+1\bigr),

and for
d\geq 3
,

2C_{1}L\epsilon\sum_{\ell=N_{1}+1}^{N_{2}}2^{-\ell(1-d/2)}\leq 2C_{1}L\epsilon 2^{(N_{2}+1)(d/2-1)}\leq 2^{d}C_{1}L\epsilon\Bigl(\frac{2M}{\epsilon}\Bigr)^{1-2/d}=2^{d+1-2/d}C_{1}LM(M^{-1}\epsilon)^{2/d}.

•

For the third term,

4MC_{1}L\sum_{\ell=N_{2}+1}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}=8MC_{1}L2^{-(N_{2}+1)}\leq 4MC_{1}L\Bigl(\frac{\epsilon}{2M}\Bigr)^{2/d}=\frac{4C_{1}}{2^{2/d}}L(M^{-1}\epsilon)^{2/d}.

Combining these upper bounds, we conclude that for an appropriate choice of
constant

C_{2}:=3\max\{4^{d}(2C_{1})^{2/d},2C_{1},2^{d+1-2/dC_{1}},4C_{1}2^{2/d}\}
,

\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C_{2}\cdot\begin{cases}L\epsilon\max\{1,1/M,\log_{2}(M/\epsilon)\},&\quad{if $d=2$}\\
L^{2/d}\epsilon^{4/(2+d)}+LM\Bigl(\frac{\epsilon}{M}\Bigr)^{2/d},&\quad{if $d\geq 3$.}\end{cases}

Since this holds for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
, it follows that the modulus of
continuity is likewise upper bounded, i.e.

\Bigl\{\Omega\Bigl(\Theta_{\infty}^{0,\infty}(2M)\cap\Theta_{\infty}^{1,1}(2L),\epsilon_{n}+\lambda\Bigr)\Bigr\}^{2}\leq C_{3}\cdot\begin{cases}L\lambda\max\{1,1/M,\log_{2}(M/\lambda)\},&\quad{if $d=2$}\\
L^{2/d}\lambda^{4/(2+d)}+LM\Bigl(\frac{\lambda}{M}\Bigr)^{2/d},&\quad{if $d\geq 3$,}\end{cases}

(S.106)

where
C_{3}:=2^{1+2/d}C_{2}
, and we recall the assumption
\lambda\geq 2\epsilon_{n}
, which implies
\epsilon_{n}+\lambda\leq 2\lambda
.
Combining (
S.100
),
(
S.103
) and
(
S.106
) gives the desired upper
bound (
S.59
).
∎

G.3.3
Proof of Lemma
S.9

We are going to show that

\mathbb{P}(\bigl|\widetilde{\theta}_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(y_{1:n})-\theta_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(f_{0})\bigr|\geq\delta_{n})\leq\frac{3\delta}{n}.

(S.107)

From (
S.107
), taking a union bound over all
\ell=0,\ldots,\log_{2}(n)/d
,
k\in\mathcal{K}(\ell)
and
{\bf i}\in\mathcal{I}
implies
the claim with
C_{4}:=2^{(d+2)}
, noting that
|\mathcal{I}|=2^{d}-1
and so

\sum_{\ell=0}^{\frac{1}{d}\log_{2}(n)}|\mathcal{I}||\mathcal{K}(\ell)|\leq 2^{d}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\frac{1}{d}\log_{2}(n)}2^{\ell}\leq 2^{(d+2)}n.

It remains to show (
S.107
). For ease of notation, in
the remainder of this proof we write
\widetilde{\theta}(\cdot)=\widetilde{\theta}_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(\cdot)
and
\theta(\cdot)=\theta_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(\cdot)
.
Decomposing
y_{i}=f_{0}(x_{i})+z_{i}
, we have

\bigl|\widetilde{\theta}(y_{1:n})-\theta(f_{0})\bigr|\leq\bigl|\widetilde{\theta}(y_{1:n})-\widetilde{\theta}(f_{0})\bigr|+\bigl|\widetilde{\theta}(f_{0})-\theta(f_{0})\bigr|,

(S.108)

and we now proceed to give high-probability upper bounds on each term
in (
S.108
). To do so, recall Bernstein’s inequality:
if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are independent, zero-mean random variables such that

|x_{i}|\leq b
and
\mathbb{E}[x_{i}^{2}]\leq\sigma^{2}
, for all
i=1,\ldots,n
, then

\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\biggr|\geq t\Biggr)\leq 2\exp\biggl(-\frac{\frac{1}{2}nt^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{3}bt}\biggr).

(S.109)

Term 1 in (
S.108
): response noise.

To upper bound
|\widetilde{\theta}(y_{1:n})-\widetilde{\theta}(f_{0})|=|\widetilde{\theta}(z_{1:n})|
, we condition on the event

\mathcal{Z}=\bigl\{\max_{i=1,\ldots,n}z_{i}\leq\sqrt{4\log(2n/\delta)}\bigr\},

which occurs with probability at least
1-\delta/n
. Note that the
following statements hold conditional on
\mathcal{Z}
:

1.

The noise variables
z_{i}
are conditionally independent,
z_{i}\perp z_{j}|\mathcal{Z}
, and have conditional mean
\mathbb{E}[z_{i}|\mathcal{Z}]=0
.

2.

The conditional variance of
z_{i}\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x_{i})
is upper bounded,

\mathrm{Var}\bigl(z_{i}\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x_{i})|\mathcal{Z}\bigr)\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl[z_{i}^{2}\bigl(\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x_{i})\bigr)^{2}|\mathcal{Z}\bigr]\leq 2\log(2n/\delta)\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x_{i})\bigr)^{2}|\mathcal{Z}\bigr]=2\log(2n/\delta),

with the last equality following from the
L^{2}(\Omega)
normalization of

\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}
, along with the independence of
x_{i}
and
z_{i}
.

3.

For each
i=1,\ldots,n
,

|z_{i}\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x_{i})|\leq\sqrt{2\log(2n/\delta)}2^{\ell d/2}\leq\sqrt{2n\log(2n/\delta)}.

We may therefore apply Bernstein’s inequality (
S.109
)
conditional on
\mathcal{Z}
, and conclude that for
\delta_{1,n}=4\log^{3/2}(2n/\delta)/\sqrt{n}
,

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(|\widetilde{\theta}(z_{1:n})|\geq\delta_{1,n}\Bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Z}^{c})+\mathbb{P}(|\widetilde{\theta}(z_{1:n})|\geq\delta_{1,n}|\mathcal{Z})\leq\frac{2\delta}{n}.

(S.110)

Term 2 in (
S.108
): empirical
coefficient.

To upper bound
|\widetilde{\theta}(f_{0})-\theta(f_{0})|
, observe that the random
variables
\Psi_{\ell k}(x_{i})f_{0}(x_{i})-\theta(f_{0})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
are mean-zero and independent. Additionally,

\mathrm{Var}\bigl(f_{0}(x_{i})\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x_{i})\bigr)\leq M^{2}

and

|f_{0}(x)\Psi_{\ell k}^{{\bf i}}(x)|\leq 2^{\ell d/2}M.

Applying Bernstein’s inequality (
S.109
) again, unconditionally
this time, we conclude that for
\delta_{2,n}=\sqrt{12}M\sqrt{\log(2n/\delta)}/\sqrt{n}
,

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(|\widetilde{\theta}(f_{0})-\theta(f_{0})|\geq\delta_{2,n}\Bigr)\leq\frac{\delta}{n}.

(S.111)

Together (
S.110
)
and (
S.111
) imply (
S.107
),
noting that
\delta_{n}=\delta_{1,n}+\delta_{2,n}
.
∎

G.3.4
Proof of Lemma
S.10

Set
f(\delta)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}A_{k}\log^{a_{k}}(b_{k}/\delta)
. We use the
identity

\mathbb{E}[X]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X>t)\,dt\leq f(1)+\int_{f(1)}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(X>t)\,dt.

(S.112)

Note
f^{-1}(f(1))=1
,
f^{-1}(\infty)=0
and

f^{\prime}(\delta)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}A_{k}a_{k}(\log b/\delta)^{a_{k}-1}/\delta
.
U-substitution with
t=f(\delta)
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X]

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}(X>f(\delta))f^{\prime}(\delta)\,d\delta

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}A_{k}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}(X>f(\delta))\frac{(\log b_{k}/\delta)^{a_{k}-1}}{\delta}\,d\delta

\displaystyle\leq B\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}A_{k}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}(\log b_{k}/\delta)^{a_{k}-1}\,d\delta\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq B\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}A_{k}\max\{2^{a_{k}},1\}\Bigl((\log b_{k})^{a_{k}-1}+\int_{0}^{1}(\log 1/\delta)^{a_{k}-1}\,d\delta\Bigr)

\displaystyle=B\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}A_{k}\max\{2^{a_{k}},1\}\Bigl((\log b_{k})^{a_{k}-1}+\Gamma(a_{k})\Bigr),

where the last inequality follows by the algebraic fact
(x+y)^{a}\leq 2^{a}(x^{a}+y^{a})
for all
a>0
, and the last equality comes from
substituting
h=\log(1/\delta)
. Combining this
with (
S.112
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}A_{k}(\log b_{k})^{a_{k}}+B\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{k}A_{k}\max\{2^{a_{k}},1\}\Bigl((\log b_{k})^{a_{k}-1}+\Gamma(a_{k})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}\sum_{k=1}^{K}A_{k}(\log b_{k})^{a_{k}}

for
C_{5}:=2B\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}\{a_{k}2^{a_{k}}\}
.
∎
